The issue is that with only 3 rows, we can put one color on a single row (collinear) and split the other 2 rows between 2 colors, each with height 1 and limited width.

To force area 3, we need a configuration where no color can be confined to a low-height region.

**Key insight:** Area 3 requires height $\geq 2$ (since $d \cdot h = 6$ and $d \geq 1$, so $h \leq 6$; but for $h = 1$, $d = 6$; for $h = 2$, $d = 3$; etc.). If a color class spans height $\geq 2$ with enough width, it might contain area 3.

But the adversary can always put one color on a single row (height 0, no triangles) and split the rest.

To prevent this, we need enough rows that one color must span $\geq 3$ rows (height $\geq 2$). With 3 colors and $n$ rows, one color spans $\geq \lceil n/3 \rceil$ rows. For height $\geq 2$, we need $\lceil n/3 \rceil \geq 3$, i.e., $n \geq 7$.

So with 7 rows, one color spans $\geq 3$ rows (height $\geq 2$). But even with height 2, the color class might not have enough points to form area 3.

Let me think about this more carefully. With 7 rows and $m$ columns, one color has $\geq \lceil 7m/3 \rceil$ points and spans $\geq 3$ rows. If the 3 rows are not consecutive, the height could be $> 2$.

Actually, "spans 3 rows" means the color appears in at least 3 rows, but the rows might not be consecutive. The height (max $y$-difference) could be up to 6.

If a color spans rows $r_1, r_2, r_3$ with $r_3 - r_1 \geq 2$, and has points in each of these rows, then we might be able to form area 3.

But the adversary can choose which rows to put the color in. For instance, the adversary might put the color in rows 0, 1, 2 (consecutive, height 2) with few points in each row.

Let me think about a specific configuration. Consider a $7 \times 7$ grid (49 points). One color has $\geq 17$ points and spans $\geq 3$ rows.

If the color spans 3 consecutive rows (height 2), it has $\geq 17$ points in 3 rows of 7 columns = 21 points. So the color has $\geq 17$ out of 21 points in these rows. That's a lot!

With 17 points in 3 rows of 7 columns, by pigeonhole, one row has $\geq 6$ points. With 6 points in a row of 7, the row has at most 1 gap. The pairwise distances include 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6 (almost all).

If the row has points at positions $\{0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5\}$ (missing 6), then distance 3 is present (e.g., 0 and 3). With a point in a row 2 away, we get area 3.

But the adversary might not put 17 points in 3 consecutive rows. The 17 points could be spread across all 7 rows.

Let me think about this more carefully. With 17 points in 7 rows of 7 columns:
- By pigeonhole, one row has $\geq 3$ points (since $17/7 > 2$).
- If a row has 3 points at positions including distance 3, and there's a point in a row at distance 2, we get area 3.

But the adversary can avoid distance 3 in each row (as I showed earlier, there are 3-element subsets of $\{0,...,6\}$ avoiding distance 3).

This is getting very complicated. Let me try a completely different approach.

**Approach: Use the $\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}$ hint with $p = 7$.**

Consider the 49 points $\{0, 1, \ldots, 6\}^2$. A 3-coloring gives one color with $\geq 17$ points.

In $(\mathbb{Z}/7\mathbb{Z})^2$, the "area" of a triangle is $D/2 \pmod{7}$ where $D = x_1(y_2 - y_3) + x_2(y_3 - y_1) + x_3(y_1 - y_2) \pmod{7}$.

For area 3 in $\mathbb{Z}^2$, $D = \pm 6 \equiv \pm 6 \pmod{7}$, i.e., $D \equiv 6$ or $D \equiv 1 \pmod{7}$.

If we can show that any 17-point subset of $(\mathbb{Z}/7\mathbb{Z})^2$ contains a triangle with $D \equiv 6$ or $D \equiv 1 \pmod{7}$, then every 3-coloring of $\mathbb{Z}^2$ has a mono triple with $D \equiv 6$ or $1 \pmod{7}$.

In $\mathbb{Z}^2$, this means $D = 6 + 7k$ or $D = 1 + 7k$ for some integer $k$. The area is $|D|/2$. For $D = 6$: area 3. For $D = 1$: area 1/2. For $D = -6 = 1 \pmod{7}$: area 3. For $D = 8 = 1 \pmod{7}$: area 4. Etc.

So the modular approach gives us area $\in \{1/2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, \ldots\}$ (from $D \equiv 1$) or area $\in \{3, 5/2, 10, \ldots\}$ (from $D \equiv 6$). Wait, let me be more careful.

$D \equiv 6 \pmod{7}$: $D \in \{\ldots, -8, -1, 6, 13, \ldots\}$. Areas: $\{\ldots, 4, 1/2, 3, 13/2, \ldots\}$.
$D \equiv 1 \pmod{7}$: $D \in \{\ldots, -6, 1, 8, \ldots\}$. Areas: $\{\ldots, 3, 1/2, 4, \ldots\}$.

So $D \equiv 6$ or $1 \pmod{7}$ gives areas $\in \{\ldots, 1/2, 3, 4, 13/2, \ldots\}$. This includes area 3 but also area 1/2 and area 4.

So the modular approach with $p = 7$ doesn't uniquely give area 3. It gives area 3 OR area 1/2 OR area 4 OR ...

But we know area 1/2 is avoidable (by $x \mod 3$). So if the modular approach guarantees a mono triangle with $D \equiv 6$ or $1 \pmod{7}$, and the coloring avoids area 1/2 (and area 4, etc.), then the triangle must have area 3.

Hmm, but the coloring might not avoid area 1/2 or area 4. We need a different approach.

**Approach: Combine modular information from multiple primes.**

If we can show that every 3-coloring has a mono triple with $D \equiv 0 \pmod{2}$ and $D \equiv 0 \pmod{3}$ (i.e., $D \equiv 0 \pmod{6}$), then $D$ is a multiple of 6, and the area is a multiple of 3. The smallest positive multiple is 3.

But can we show $D \equiv 0 \pmod{6}$ for some mono triple?

$D \equiv 0 \pmod{2}$: This means the doubled area is even, i.e., the area is an integer. The coloring $(x \mod 2) + (y \mod 2)$ ensures this for all mono triples. But a general coloring doesn't.

$D \equiv 0 \pmod{3}$: The coloring $x \mod 3$ ensures this. But a general coloring doesn't.

So for a general coloring, we can't guarantee $D \equiv 0 \pmod{2}$ or $D \equiv 0 \pmod{3}$ individually, let alone both.

Hmm. Let me think about this differently.

**Actually, let me reconsider the problem. Maybe the answer is not 3.**

Let me reconsider. I've been assuming the answer is 3, but I haven't been able to prove it. Let me think about whether there might be a coloring that avoids area 3.

The key colorings I've found:
1. $c(x,y) = x \mod 3$: mono areas are multiples of $3/2$.
2. $c(x,y) = (x \mod 2) + (y \mod 2)$: mono areas are integers.

For coloring 1, the mono areas include $3/2, 3, 9/2, 6, \ldots$. So area 3 is achievable.
For coloring 2, the mono areas include $1, 2, 3, 4, \ldots$. So area 3 is achievable.

Can we find a coloring where the mono areas avoid 3?

I need a coloring where no mono triple has $|D| = 6$.

Let me think about what structural property a coloring needs to avoid $|D| = 6$.

$|D| = 6$ with $D = x_1(y_2 - y_3) + x_2(y_3 - y_1) + x_3(y_1 - y_2)$.

This is equivalent to: the three points form a triangle with doubled area 6.

The sublattices of index 6 in $\mathbb{Z}^2$ are:
- $\langle (1, 0), (0, 6) \rangle$, $\langle (1, 1), (0, 6) \rangle$, ..., $\langle (1, 5), (0, 6) \rangle$ (6 sublattices)
- $\langle (2, 0), (0, 3) \rangle$, $\langle (2, 1), (0, 3) \rangle$, $\langle (2, 2), (0, 3) \rangle$ (3 sublattices)
- $\langle (3, 0), (0, 2) \rangle$, $\langle (3, 1), (0, 2) \rangle$ (2 sublattices)
- $\langle (6, 0), (0, 1) \rangle$ (1 sublattice)

Each sublattice $L$ gives area-3 triangles: for each coset $A + L$, the triangle $(A, A + u, A + v)$ has area 3, where $(u, v)$ is the HNF basis.

To avoid area 3, for each sublattice $L$ and each coset $A + L$, the three points $A, A + u, A + v$ must not all be the same color.

This is equivalent to: for each sublattice $L$ of index 6, the coloring restricted to each coset of $L$ must not be constant on the triangle $(0, u, v)$ (up to translation).

This is a very strong condition. Let me think about whether it's satisfiable.

**Observation:** Consider the sublattice $L = \langle (6, 0), (0, 1) \rangle$. The cosets are $\{(a, y) : x \equiv a \pmod{6}\}$ for $a = 0, 1, \ldots, 5$. For each coset, the triangle is $(a, y), (a+6, y), (a, y+1)$, which has $D = 6$. To avoid area 3, for each $a$ and $y$, the points $(a, y), (a+6, y), (a, y+1)$ must not be monochromatic.

This means: for each $a$, the coloring on the "column" $x \equiv a \pmod{6}$ must satisfy: $(a, y)$ and $(a+6, y)$ and $(a, y+1)$ are not all the same color. Since $(a, y)$ and $(a+6, y)$ are in the same coset, this is a condition on the coloring within each coset.

Similarly, for the sublattice $L = \langle (1, 0), (0, 6) \rangle$, the cosets are $\{(x, b) : y \equiv b \pmod{6}\}$. For each coset, the triangle is $(x, b), (x+1, b), (x, b+6)$, which has $D = 6$. To avoid area 3, $(x, b), (x+1, b), (x, b+6)$ must not be monochromatic.

And for $L = \langle (2, 0), (0, 3) \rangle$, the triangle is $(a, b), (a+2, b), (a, b+3)$ with $D = 6$.

And for $L = \langle (3, 0), (0, 2) \rangle$, the triangle is $(a, b), (a+3, b), (a, b+2)$ with $D = 6$.

And for $L = \langle (1, b), (0, 6) \rangle$ for $b = 0, \ldots, 5$, the triangle is $(a, c), (a+1, c+b), (a, c+6)$ with $D = 6$.

And so on for all 12 sublattices.

This gives a huge number of constraints. It seems very hard to satisfy all of them simultaneously.

**Let me try to prove that area 3 is unavoidable by contradiction.**

Suppose there exists a 3-coloring that avoids area 3. Then for every sublattice $L$ of index 6 and every coset $A + L$, the triangle $(A, A+u, A+v)$ is not monochromatic.

Consider the sublattice $L_0 = \langle (3, 0), (0, 2) \rangle$. The cosets are $\{(x, y) : x \equiv a \pmod{3}, y \equiv b \pmod{2}\}$ for $(a, b) \in \{0, 1, 2\} \times \{0, 1\}$, giving 6 cosets.

For each coset, the triangle is $(a + 3i, b + 2j), (a + 3(i+1), b + 2j), (a + 3i, b + 2(j+1))$, which has $D = 6$. To avoid area 3, these three points must not be monochromatic.

This means: for each $(a, b)$, and for each $(i, j)$, the points $(a+3i, b+2j), (a+3(i+1), b+2j), (a+3i, b+2(j+1))$ are not all the same color.

This is a local condition on the coloring within each coset. The coset is a sublattice isomorphic to $\mathbb{Z}^2$ (via the map $(i, j) \mapsto (a+3i, b+2j)$). The condition is: for each $(i, j)$, the points $(i, j), (i+1, j), (i, j+1)$ (in the sublattice coordinates) are not monochromatic.

This is the condition that the coloring on $\mathbb{Z}^2$ (the sublattice) has no monochromatic "corner" of the form $(i, j), (i+1, j), (i, j+1)$.

But this is exactly the condition for avoiding monochromatic corners, which is known to be impossible for any finite coloring of $\mathbb{Z}^2$!

**Theorem (Ajtalai–Szemerédi, 1974):** For any $r$-coloring of $\mathbb{Z}^2$ and any $\epsilon > 0$, there exists a monochromatic corner $(x, y), (x+d, y), (x, y+d)$ for some $d > 0$.

Wait, the corners theorem is about corners of the form $(x, y), (x+d, y), (x, y+d)$ with the SAME $d$. Our condition is about corners with $d = 1$ in the sublattice coordinates, which corresponds to $(a+3i, b+2j), (a+3(i+1), b+2j), (a+3i, b+2(j+1))$ in the original coordinates.

The corners theorem guarantees a monochromatic corner for SOME $d$, not necessarily $d = 1$. So it doesn't directly apply.

But there's a stronger result:

**Theorem (Gallai on $\mathbb{Z}^2$):** For any finite coloring of $\mathbb{Z}^2$ and any finite set $S \subset \mathbb{Z}^2$, there exists a monochromatic homothetic copy $a + d \cdot S$ for some $a \in \mathbb{Z}^2$ and positive integer $d$.

Applying this to $S = \{(0,0), (1,0), (0,1)\}$ (a corner with $d = 1$), we get a monochromatic corner $(a, b), (a+d, b), (a, b+d)$ for some $d > 0$.

In the sublattice coordinates, this gives a monochromatic corner with step $d$ (not necessarily 1). The area of this corner (in the original coordinates) is $\frac{1}{2} |(3d)(2d)| = 3d^2$. For $d = 1$: area 3. For $d = 2$: area 12. Etc.

So Gallai's theorem gives us a monochromatic triangle of area $3d^2$ for some $d \geq 1$. This includes area 3 (when $d = 1$), but doesn't guarantee $d = 1$.

Hmm, so Gallai's theorem alone doesn't give us area exactly 3.

But wait, we need to be more careful. Gallai's theorem applies to the coloring of the sublattice (which is a 3-coloring of $\mathbb{Z}^2$). It gives a monochromatic homothetic copy of $\{(0,0), (1,0), (0,1)\}$ in the sublattice, which is a corner $(i, j), (i+d, j), (i, j+d)$ in sublattice coordinates. In original coordinates, this is $(a+3i, b+2j), (a+3(i+d), b+2j), (a+3i, b+2(j+d))$, which has $D = (3d)(2d) = 6d^2$, area $= 3d^2$.

So we get area $3d^2$ for some $d \geq 1$, which is $3, 12, 27, \ldots$. This includes 3 but doesn't guarantee it.

**But we have 6 cosets, and Gallai's theorem applies to each coset independently.** Each coset is a 3-coloring of $\mathbb{Z}^2$, so each has a monochromatic corner with some step $d$. But the step $d$ might be different for different cosets, and might be $> 1$ for all of them.

So this approach doesn't directly give area 3.

**Let me think about this differently.**

Actually, wait. The condition for avoiding area 3 is not just about the sublattice $L_0 = \langle (3, 0), (0, 2) \rangle$. It's about ALL 12 sublattices of index 6. Each sublattice gives a different set of constraints.

For the sublattice $L_0 = \langle (3, 0), (0, 2) \rangle$: avoid monochromatic $(i, j), (i+1, j), (i, j+1)$ in each coset.
For the sublattice $L_1 = \langle (6, 0), (0, 1) \rangle$: avoid monochromatic $(i, j), (i+1, j), (i, j+1)$ in each coset (but the sublattice has different structure).
For the sublattice $L_2 = \langle (2, 0), (0, 3) \rangle$: avoid monochromatic $(i, j), (i+1, j), (i, j+1)$ in each coset.
Etc.

Each sublattice gives a different "corner" condition on a different set of cosets. The combination of all these conditions might be impossible to satisfy.

**Key insight:** The sublattice $L_0 = \langle (3, 0), (0, 2) \rangle$ has 6 cosets. Each coset is a copy of $\mathbb{Z}^2$ with a 3-coloring. By Gallai's theorem, each coset has a monochromatic corner with some step $d$. If $d = 1$ for any coset, we get area 3.

But what if $d > 1$ for all 6 cosets? Then we get area $3d^2 > 3$ for each coset, but not area 3.

However, the sublattice $L_1 = \langle (1, 0), (0, 6) \rangle$ gives a different set of constraints. For $L_1$, the cosets are $\{(x, y) : y \equiv b \pmod{6}\}$ for $b = 0, \ldots, 5$. The triangle is $(x, b), (x+1, b), (x, b+6)$, which has $D = 6$. In sublattice coordinates (mapping $(x, y) \to (x, (y-b)/6)$), the corner is $(i, j), (i+1, j), (i, j+1)$, and the area in original coordinates is $\frac{1}{2} |1 \cdot 6| = 3$ (for $d = 1$).

So for $L_1$, Gallai's theorem gives a monochromatic corner with step $d$, and the area is $\frac{1}{2}|d \cdot 6d| = 3d^2$. Same as before.

**The issue is that Gallai's theorem gives step $d \geq 1$ but doesn't guarantee $d = 1$.**

To get $d = 1$, we need a stronger result. Specifically, we need:

**Claim:** For any 3-coloring of $\mathbb{Z}^2$, there exists a monochromatic corner $(x, y), (x+1, y), (x, y+1)$ (with step $d = 1$).

Is this true? This is the question of whether the "unit corner" is Ramsey.

Actually, this is NOT true in general. The coloring $c(x, y) = (x + y) \mod 3$ avoids monochromatic unit corners: $(x, y), (x+1, y), (x, y+1)$ have colors $x+y, x+1+y, x+y+1$, which are $c, c+1, c+1 \pmod{3}$. If $c = 0$: colors $0, 1, 1$. Not mono. If $c = 1$: $1, 2, 2$. Not mono. If $c = 2$: $2, 0, 0$. Not mono. So no monochromatic unit corner.

So the unit corner is NOT Ramsey for 3 colors. This means the approach via $L_0$ and Gallai's theorem doesn't give area 3 directly.

But we have 12 different sublattices, each giving a different corner condition. Even if the unit corner is avoidable for one sublattice, it might not be avoidable for all 12 simultaneously.

**Let me think about this more carefully.**

For the sublattice $L_0 = \langle (3, 0), (0, 2) \rangle$, the condition is: no monochromatic $(a+3i, b+2j), (a+3(i+1), b+2j), (a+3i, b+2(j+1))$ for any $(a, b)$ coset representative and any $(i, j)$.

This is equivalent to: the coloring $c$ restricted to each coset of $L_0$ has no monochromatic unit corner (in sublattice coordinates).

The coloring $c(x, y) = (x + y) \mod 3$ on a coset of $L_0$: the coset is $\{(a + 3i, b + 2j) : i, j \in \mathbb{Z}\}$. The color of $(a + 3i, b + 2j)$ is $(a + 3i + b + 2j) \mod 3 = (a + b + 2j) \mod 3$ (since $3i \equiv 0$). So within a coset, the color depends only on $j$ (not on $i$). Specifically, $c = (a + b + 2j) \mod 3$.

A unit corner in the coset: $(i, j), (i+1, j), (i, j+1)$, with colors $(a+b+2j), (a+b+2j), (a+b+2(j+1)) = (a+b+2j), (a+b+2j), (a+b+2j+2)$. The first two are the same, the third is different (since $2 \not\equiv 0 \pmod{3}$). So no monochromatic unit corner. ✓

So the coloring $c(x, y) = (x + y) \mod 3$ avoids monochromatic unit corners for the sublattice $L_0$. But does it avoid area 3 entirely?

For $L_0$, the area-3 triangles are $(a+3i, b+2j), (a+3(i+1), b+2j), (a+3i, b+2(j+1))$ with $D = 6$. The coloring gives colors $(a+b+2j), (a+b+2j), (a+b+2j+2)$. Since the first two are the same but the third is different, this is not monochromatic. ✓

But we need to check all 12 sublattices. Let me check $L_1 = \langle (1, 0), (0, 6) \rangle$. The cosets are $\{(x, b + 6j) : x \in \mathbb{Z}, j \in \mathbb{Z}\}$ for $b = 0, \ldots, 5$. The triangle is $(x, b+6j), (x+1, b+6j), (x, b+6(j+1))$ with $D = 6$.

Colors: $(x + b + 6j), (x + 1 + b + 6j), (x + b + 6(j+1)) = (x + b + 6j), (x + b + 6j + 1), (x + b + 6j + 6)$. Modulo 3: $(x + b), (x + b + 1), (x + b)$. The first and third are the same, the second is different. Not monochromatic. ✓

Check $L_2 = \langle (2, 0), (0, 3) \rangle$. Cosets: $\{(a + 2i, b + 3j)\}$ for $(a, b) \in \{0, 1\} \times \{0, 1, 2\}$. Triangle: $(a+2i, b+3j), (a+2(i+1), b+3j), (a+2i, b+3(j+1))$ with $D = 6$.

Colors: $(a + 2i + b + 3j), (a + 2(i+1) + b + 3j), (a + 2i + b + 3(j+1))$. Mod 3: $(a + 2i + b), (a + 2i + 2 + b), (a + 2i + b)$. First and third are the same, second is different (since $2 \not\equiv 0$). Not monochromatic. ✓

Check $L_3 = \langle (6, 0), (0, 1) \rangle$. Cosets: $\{(a + 6i, y) : i \in \mathbb{Z}, y \in \mathbb{Z}\}$ for $a = 0, \ldots, 5$. Triangle: $(a+6i, y), (a+6(i+1), y), (a+6i, y+1)$ with $D = 6$.

Colors: $(a + 6i + y), (a + 6(i+1) + y), (a + 6i + y + 1)$. Mod 3: $(a + y), (a + y), (a + y + 1)$. First two same, third different. Not monochromatic. ✓

Check $L_4 = \langle (1, 1), (0, 6) \rangle$. HNF: $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 1 & 6 \end{pmatrix}$, det = 6. Triangle: $(x, y), (x+1, y+1), (x, y+6)$ with $D = 1 \cdot 6 - 0 \cdot 1 = 6$. 

Colors: $(x+y), (x+1+y+1), (x+y+6)$. Mod 3: $(x+y), (x+y+2), (x+y)$. First and third same, second different. Not monochromatic. ✓

Check $L_5 = \langle (1, 2), (0, 6) \rangle$. Triangle: $(x, y), (x+1, y+2), (x, y+6)$ with $D = 6$.

Colors: $(x+y), (x+1+y+2), (x+y+6)$. Mod 3: $(x+y), (x+y+3), (x+y)$. All the same mod 3! So this IS monochromatic for the coloring $c = (x+y) \mod 3$.

Wait, let me recheck. $(x+1+y+2) = (x+y+3) \equiv (x+y) \pmod{3}$. And $(x+y+6) \equiv (x+y) \pmod{3}$. So all three are the same color!

So the coloring $c(x, y) = (x + y) \mod 3$ does NOT avoid area 3 for the sublattice $L_5 = \langle (1, 2), (0, 6) \rangle$.

Specifically, the triangle $(x, y), (x+1, y+2), (x, y+6)$ is monochromatic. For example, $(0, 0), (1, 2), (0, 6)$: colors $0, 3 \equiv 0, 6 \equiv 0$. All color 0. $D = 0(2-6) + 1(6-0) + 0(0-2) = 6$. Area = 3. ✓

So the coloring $(x+y) \mod 3$ does NOT avoid area 3. Good, this is consistent with what I found earlier.

Now, the question is: is there ANY 3-coloring that avoids area 3 for ALL 12 sublattices?

For each sublattice $L_k$, the condition is that the coloring on each coset of $L_k$ has no monochromatic "unit corner" (in the sublattice coordinates). This is 12 different corner-avoidance conditions, each on a different set of cosets.

The coloring $(x+y) \mod 3$ avoids the corner condition for $L_0, L_1, L_2, L_3, L_4$ but not for $L_5$.

Can we find a coloring that avoids the corner condition for ALL 12 sublattices?

This seems very hard. Let me think about whether it's possible.

**Key observation:** The 12 sublattices of index 6 correspond to the 12 ways to write $6 = ad$ with $a, d > 0$ and $0 \leq b < d$. Each sublattice gives a different "corner" shape.

For a coloring to avoid area 3, it must avoid monochromatic corners of all 12 shapes simultaneously. This is a very restrictive condition.

**Let me try to prove that no 3-coloring can avoid all 12 corner conditions.**

Actually, let me think about this more carefully. The 12 sublattices give 12 different "corner" shapes. But some of these shapes might be equivalent under the coloring's symmetry.

Let me focus on a few key sublattices:

1. $L_0 = \langle (3, 0), (0, 2) \rangle$: corner $(0,0), (3,0), (0,2)$. Condition: no mono $(x, y), (x+3, y), (x, y+2)$.
2. $L_2 = \langle (2, 0), (0, 3) \rangle$: corner $(0,0), (2,0), (0,3)$. Condition: no mono $(x, y), (x+2, y), (x, y+3)$.
3. $L_3 = \langle (6, 0), (0, 1) \rangle$: corner $(0,0), (6,0), (0,1)$. Condition: no mono $(x, y), (x+6, y), (x, y+1)$.
4. $L_1 = \langle (1, 0), (0, 6) \rangle$: corner $(0,0), (1,0), (0,6)$. Condition: no mono $(x, y), (x+1, y), (x, y+6)$.

And the "tilted" sublattices:
5. $L_4 = \langle (1, 1), (0, 6) \rangle$: corner $(0,0), (1,1), (0,6)$. Condition: no mono $(x, y), (x+1, y+1), (x, y+6)$.
6. $L_5 = \langle (1, 2), (0, 6) \rangle$: corner $(0,0), (1,2), (0,6)$. Condition: no mono $(x, y), (x+1, y+2), (x, y+6)$.
...

Now, conditions 1 and 2 are particularly interesting. Condition 1 says: no mono $(x, y), (x+3, y), (x, y+2)$. Condition 2 says: no mono $(x, y), (x+2, y), (x, y+3)$.

These are "corner" conditions with different step sizes. The question is whether a 3-coloring can avoid both.

**Let me try the coloring $c(x, y) = (ax + by) \mod 3$ for various $a, b$.**

For condition 1 (no mono $(x, y), (x+3, y), (x, y+2)$):
Colors: $ax + by, a(x+3) + by, ax + b(y+2) = ax+by, ax+by+3a, ax+by+2b$. Mod 3: $c, c, c + 2b$. Monochromatic iff $2b \equiv 0 \pmod{3}$, i.e., $b \equiv 0 \pmod{3}$.

For condition 2 (no mono $(x, y), (x+2, y), (x, y+3)$):
Colors: $c, c + 2a, c + 3b$. Mod 3: $c, c + 2a, c$. Monochromatic iff $2a \equiv 0 \pmod{3}$, i.e., $a \equiv 0 \pmod{3}$.

So for a linear coloring $c = (ax + by) \mod 3$:
- Condition 1 is satisfied (avoids mono) iff $b \not\equiv 0 \pmod{3}$.
- Condition 2 is satisfied iff $a \not\equiv 0 \pmod{3}$.

So we need $a \not\equiv 0$ and $b \not\equiv 0 \pmod{3}$. E.g., $a = 1, b = 1$: $c = (x + y) \mod 3$.

But we already showed that this coloring fails condition 6 (sublattice $L_5$). Let me check:

Condition 6 (no mono $(x, y), (x+1, y+2), (x, y+6)$):
Colors: $(x+y), (x+1+y+2), (x+y+6)$. Mod 3: $(x+y), (x+y+3), (x+y+6) \equiv (x+y), (x+y), (x+y)$. Monochromatic! ✗

So $c = (x+y) \mod 3$ fails condition 6.

Let me try $a = 1, b = 2$: $c = (x + 2y) \mod 3$.

Condition 1: $2b = 4 \equiv 1 \not\equiv 0$. ✓ (avoids mono)
Condition 2: $2a = 2 \not\equiv 0$. ✓
Condition 3 (no mono $(x, y), (x+6, y), (x, y+1)$): Colors: $c, c + 6a = c, c + b = c + 2$. Mod 3: $c, c, c+2$. Not mono. ✓
Condition 4 (no mono $(x, y), (x+1, y), (x, y+6)$): Colors: $c, c + a = c + 1, c + 6b = c$. Mod 3: $c, c+1, c$. Not mono. ✓
Condition 5 (no mono $(x, y), (x+1, y+1), (x, y+6)$): Colors: $c, c + a + b = c + 3 \equiv c, c + 6b \equiv c$. Mod 3: $c, c, c$. Mono! ✗

So $c = (x + 2y) \mod 3$ fails condition 5.

Let me try $a = 2, b = 1$: $c = (2x + y) \mod 3$.

Condition 1: $2b = 2 \not\equiv 0$. ✓
Condition 2: $2a = 4 \equiv 1 \not\equiv 0$. ✓
Condition 3: Colors: $c, c + 6 \cdot 2 = c, c + 1$. Not mono. ✓
Condition 4: Colors: $c, c + 2, c + 6 \equiv c$. Mod 3: $c, c+2, c$. Not mono. ✓
Condition 5: Colors: $c, c + 2 + 1 = c + 3 \equiv c, c + 6 \equiv c$. Mod 3: $c, c, c$. Mono! ✗

Same issue. Condition 5 fails for $a + b \equiv 0 \pmod{3}$, which happens when $a = 2, b = 1$ (since $2 + 1 = 3 \equiv 0$).

For condition 5 to be satisfied, we need $a + b \not\equiv 0 \pmod{3}$.

So we need: $a \not\equiv 0$, $b \not\equiv 0$, $a + b \not\equiv 0 \pmod{3}$. With $a, b \in \{1, 2\}$:
- $a = 1, b = 1$: $a + b = 2 \not\equiv 0$. ✓
- $a = 1, b = 2$: $a + b = 3 \equiv 0$. ✗
- $a = 2, b = 1$: $a + b = 3 \equiv 0$. ✗
- $a = 2, b = 2$: $a + b = 4 \equiv 1 \not\equiv 0$. ✓

So $a = 1, b = 1$ and $a = 2, b = 2$ satisfy conditions 1-5. Let me check condition 6 for $a = 1, b = 1$ (i.e., $c = (x + y) \mod 3$):

Condition 6 (no mono $(x, y), (x+1, y+2), (x, y+6)$): Colors: $c, c + 1 + 2 = c + 3 \equiv c, c + 6 \equiv c$. Mono! ✗

And for $a = 2, b = 2$ (i.e., $c = (2x + 2y) \mod 3 = 2(x+y) \mod 3$): This is the same coloring as $(x+y) \mod 3$ up to relabeling. So same result. ✗

Now let me check condition 6 for general $a, b$:
Colors: $c, c + a + 2b, c + 6b \equiv c$. Mod 3: $c, c + a + 2b, c$. Mono iff $a + 2b \equiv 0 \pmod{3}$.

For $a = 1, b = 1$: $a + 2b = 3 \equiv 0$. ✗
For $a = 2, b = 2$: $a + 2b = 6 \equiv 0$. ✗

So for ALL linear colorings $(ax + by) \mod 3$ with $a, b \not\equiv 0$ and $a + b \not\equiv 0$, we have $a + 2b \equiv 0$ (since $a \in \{1, 2\}, b \in \{1, 2\}, a + b \not\equiv 0$ implies $a = b$, and then $a + 2b = 3a \equiv 0$).

So ALL linear colorings mod 3 fail at least one of the 12 conditions! This means no linear coloring mod 3 can avoid area 3.

But what about non-linear colorings? Can a non-linear 3-coloring avoid all 12 conditions?

This is the key question. Let me think about whether a non-linear coloring can work.

**Observation:** The 12 conditions are:
For each sublattice $L_k$ of index 6, the coloring on each coset must avoid monochromatic unit corners.

Each coset is a copy of $\mathbb{Z}^2$, and the condition is that the 3-coloring of this copy has no monochromatic unit corner.

A 3-coloring of $\mathbb{Z}^2$ with no monochromatic unit corner exists (e.g., $(x + y) \mod 3$). But the same coloring must work for all 12 sublattices simultaneously, which means it must avoid unit corners in 12 different "coordinate systems" at once.

This is a very strong condition. Let me think about whether it's possible.

**For the sublattice $L_0 = \langle (3, 0), (0, 2) \rangle$**, the condition is: no mono $(x, y), (x+3, y), (x, y+2)$. This is a "corner" with steps $(3, 0)$ and $(0, 2)$.

**For the sublattice $L_5 = \langle (1, 2), (0, 6) \rangle$**, the condition is: no mono $(x, y), (x+1, y+2), (x, y+6)$. This is a "corner" with steps $(1, 2)$ and $(0, 6)$.

These are very different shapes. A coloring that avoids one might not avoid the other.

**Let me think about this more abstractly.**

A 3-coloring $c: \mathbb{Z}^2 \to \{0, 1, 2\}$ avoids area 3 iff for every $(x, y)$ and every pair $(u, v)$ with $|\det(u, v)| = 6$ and $\gcd(u_1, u_2, v_1, v_2) = 1$, the three points $(x, y), (x, y) + u, (x, y) + v$ are not monochromatic.

This is equivalent to: for every such $(u, v)$, the coloring $c$ has no monochromatic "corner" with steps $u$ and $v$.

The number of such $(u, v)$ pairs (up to $GL_2(\mathbb{Z})$ equivalence) is 12 (the 12 sublattices). But each sublattice has infinitely many cosets, so the total number of constraints is infinite.

**Can a 3-coloring satisfy all these constraints?**

This is equivalent to asking: does there exist a 3-coloring of $\mathbb{Z}^2$ such that for every $(u, v)$ with $|\det(u, v)| = 6$ and $\gcd = 1$, there is no monochromatic triple $\{P, P+u, P+v\}$?

This is a very strong Ramsey-avoidance condition. Let me think about whether it's possible.

**Approach: Use the density Hales-Jewett theorem or similar.**

Actually, I think the key insight is that the 12 sublattices "cover" all possible directions, making it impossible to avoid all of them simultaneously.

Let me think about a specific argument.

**Argument:** Consider the 6 sublattices $L_b = \langle (1, b), (0, 6) \rangle$ for $b = 0, 1, \ldots, 5$. Each gives the condition: no mono $(x, y), (x+1, y+b), (x, y+6)$.

For a coloring $c$, the condition for $L_b$ is: $c(x, y), c(x+1, y+b), c(x, y+6)$ are not all equal, for all $(x, y)$.

Now, consider the 6 conditions for $b = 0, 1, \ldots, 5$. For each $(x, y)$, we have:
- $c(x, y) \neq c(x+1, y)$ or $c(x, y) \neq c(x, y+6)$ (from $b = 0$)
- $c(x, y) \neq c(x+1, y+1)$ or $c(x, y) \neq c(x, y+6)$ (from $b = 1$)
- ...
- $c(x, y) \neq c(x+1, y+5)$ or $c(x, y) \neq c(x, y+6)$ (from $b = 5$)

If $c(x, y) = c(x, y+6)$, then all 6 conditions require $c(x, y) \neq c(x+1, y+b)$ for $b = 0, 1, \ldots, 5$. But $c(x+1, y+b)$ for $b = 0, \ldots, 5$ takes 6 values, each in $\{0, 1, 2\}$. By pigeonhole, at least 2 of these 6 values are equal. But we need ALL 6 to be different from $c(x, y)$. Since there are only 2 other colors, at most 2 of the 6 values can be different from $c(x, y)$... wait, no. We need $c(x+1, y+b) \neq c(x, y)$ for all $b = 0, \ldots, 5$. This means all 6 values $c(x+1, y), c(x+1, y+1), \ldots, c(x+1, y+5)$ must be in $\{0, 1, 2\} \setminus \{c(x, y)\}$, which has 2 elements. By pigeonhole, at least 3 of the 6 values are the same. But that's fine — they just need to be different from $c(x, y)$, not from each other.

Actually, the condition is just that $c(x+1, y+b) \neq c(x, y)$ for all $b = 0, \ldots, 5$. This is possible: just make $c(x+1, y+b) \in \{0, 1, 2\} \setminus \{c(x, y)\}$ for all $b$. Since there are 2 choices for each $b$, this is possible.

So the condition $c(x, y) = c(x, y+6)$ doesn't immediately lead to a contradiction.

But we also have conditions from other sublattices. Let me consider the sublattices $L'_a = \langle (a, 0), (0, 6/a) \rangle$... hmm, this doesn't quite work since $6/a$ must be an integer.

Let me try a different approach.

**Approach: Consider the sublattices $\langle (3, 0), (0, 2) \rangle$ and $\langle (2, 0), (0, 3) \rangle$ simultaneously.**

Condition from $L_0 = \langle (3, 0), (0, 2) \rangle$: no mono $(x, y), (x+3, y), (x, y+2)$.
Condition from $L_2 = \langle (2, 0), (0, 3) \rangle$: no mono $(x, y), (x+2, y), (x, y+3)$.

Consider the 6 points: $(0, 0), (2, 0), (3, 0), (0, 2), (0, 3), (2, 3)$... hmm, let me think about this more carefully.

Actually, let me consider the point $(0, 0)$ and its color $c(0, 0) = \alpha$.

From $L_0$: $(0, 0), (3, 0), (0, 2)$ not mono. So either $c(3, 0) \neq \alpha$ or $c(0, 2) \neq \alpha$.
From $L_2$: $(0, 0), (2, 0), (0, 3)$ not mono. So either $c(2, 0) \neq \alpha$ or $c(0, 3) \neq \alpha$.

This gives constraints but doesn't immediately lead to a contradiction.

**Let me try a more computational approach.** Let me consider a finite set of points and check if a 3-coloring avoiding area 3 exists.

Consider the set $F = \{0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5\} \times \{0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5\}$, a $6 \times 6$ grid with 36 points. Can we 3-color this to avoid all area-3 triangles?

This is a constraint satisfaction problem with 36 variables and many constraints. It's hard to solve by hand, but let me try to reason about it.

The area-3 triangles in this grid include all triangles with $|D| = 6$ and vertices in $\{0, \ldots, 5\}^2$. There are many such triangles.

Earlier, I showed that the coloring "rows 0-1 → color 0, rows 2-3 → color 1, rows 4-5 → color 2" avoids area 3 (each color is in 2 rows, max area with height 1 is 5/2 < 3). So a $6 \times 6$ grid can be colored to avoid area 3.

What about a $6 \times 7$ grid? With 7 rows, one color must span $\geq 3$ rows. But as I showed, even with 3 rows, if the width is limited, area 3 might be avoidable.

Actually, with 3 rows and width 6 (the $6 \times 3$ subgrid), the max area with height 2 is $5 \cdot 2 / 2 = 5$. So area 3 is achievable. But can we choose points to avoid it?

In a $6 \times 3$ subgrid with all 18 points, area-3 triangles exist (e.g., $(0, 0), (3, 0), (0, 2)$). So if a color class contains all 18 points of a $6 \times 3$ subgrid, it has area 3. But the color class might not contain all 18 points.

With 3 colors and 42 points ($6 \times 7$ grid), one color has $\geq 14$ points. If these 14 points are in 3 rows (21 points available), the color has 14 out of 21 points. Can 14 points in a $6 \times 3$ subgrid avoid area 3?

The maximum area-3-free subset of a $6 \times 3$ grid... this is a finite combinatorial problem. Let me think about it.

In a $6 \times 3$ grid ($\{0,...,5\} \times \{0,1,2\}$), the area-3 triangles include:
- $(a, 0), (a+3, 0), (b, 2)$ for $a \in \{0, 1, 2\}$, $b \in \{0,...,5\}$: 18 triangles.
- $(a, 2), (a+3, 2), (b, 0)$ for $a \in \{0, 1, 2\}$, $b \in \{0,...,5\}$: 18 triangles.
- $(a, 0), (a+3, 1), (a, 2)$ for $a \in \{0, 1, 2\}$: 3 triangles.
- $(a, 0), (a+6, 0), (b, 1)$: $a + 6 > 5$, so none.
- And more...

Actually, let me also include triangles with one point on each row:
- $(a, 0), (b, 1), (c, 2)$ with $|-a + 2b - c| = 6$. With $a, c \in \{0,...,5\}$, $b \in \{0,...,5\}$: $-a + 2b - c = 6$ requires $2b = 6 + a + c \leq 16$, so $b \leq 8$, but $b \leq 5$. $a + c = 0, b = 3$: $(0,0), (3,1), (0,2)$. $a + c = 2, b = 4$: $(0,0), (4,1), (2,2)$, $(1,0), (4,1), (1,2)$, $(2,0), (4,1), (0,2)$. $a + c = 4, b = 5$: $(0,0), (5,1), (4,2)$, $(1,0), (5,1), (3,2)$, $(2,0), (5,1), (2,2)$, $(3,0), (5,1), (1,2)$, $(4,0), (5,1), (0,2)$. And $-a + 2b - c = -6$ similarly.

So there are many area-3 triangles. The maximum area-3-free subset might be small.

But I can't easily compute this by hand. Let me try a different approach.

**Let me go back to the theoretical approach.**

I've shown that no linear coloring mod 3 can avoid area 3 (they all fail at least one of the 12 sublattice conditions). Can a non-linear coloring avoid area 3?

**Key insight:** The 12 sublattice conditions are very restrictive. Let me show that they're mutually contradictory.

Consider the 6 sublattices $L_b = \langle (1, b), (0, 6) \rangle$ for $b = 0, 1, \ldots, 5$. Each gives the condition: for all $(x, y)$, $c(x, y), c(x+1, y+b), c(x, y+6)$ are not all equal.

Now, fix $(x, y)$ and consider the 6 conditions for $b = 0, \ldots, 5$:
- $c(x, y), c(x+1, y), c(x, y+6)$ not all equal.
- $c(x, y), c(x+1, y+1), c(x, y+6)$ not all equal.
- ...
- $c(x, y), c(x+1, y+5), c(x, y+6)$ not all equal.

If $c(x, y) = c(x, y+6) = \alpha$, then all 6 conditions require $c(x+1, y+b) \neq \alpha$ for $b = 0, \ldots, 5$. This means the 6 points $(x+1, y), (x+1, y+1), \ldots, (x+1, y+5)$ all have color $\neq \alpha$, i.e., they're colored with the other 2 colors.

Now, consider the sublattices $L'_a = \langle (6, 0), (0, 1) \rangle$... wait, this is $L_3$ with the condition: no mono $(x, y), (x+6, y), (x, y+1)$.

If $c(x, y) = c(x+6, y) = \alpha$, then $c(x, y+1) \neq \alpha$.

And the sublattice $L_0 = \langle (3, 0), (0, 2) \rangle$ with condition: no mono $(x, y), (x+3, y), (x, y+2)$.

If $c(x, y) = c(x+3, y) = \alpha$, then $c(x, y+2) \neq \alpha$.

And $L_2 = \langle (2, 0), (0, 3) \rangle$ with condition: no mono $(x, y), (x+2, y), (x, y+3)$.

If $c(x, y) = c(x+2, y) = \alpha$, then $c(x, y+3) \neq \alpha$.

These conditions interact in complex ways. Let me try to derive a contradiction.

**Attempt at contradiction:**

Consider the coloring on the vertical line $x = 0$. The points $(0, y)$ for $y \in \mathbb{Z}$ are 3-colored. By the pigeonhole principle, one color appears infinitely often. Say color $\alpha$ appears at $y = y_1, y_2, \ldots$.

Now, consider the condition from $L_3 = \langle (6, 0), (0, 1) \rangle$: no mono $(x, y), (x+6, y), (x, y+1)$. If $c(0, y_i) = c(6, y_i) = \alpha$, then $c(0, y_i + 1) \neq \alpha$.

And from $L_b = \langle (1, b), (0, 6) \rangle$: if $c(0, y) = c(0, y+6) = \alpha$, then $c(1, y+b) \neq \alpha$ for all $b = 0, \ldots, 5$.

This means: if two points on the vertical line $x = 0$ at distance 6 have color $\alpha$, then the 6 points $(1, y), (1, y+1), \ldots, (1, y+5)$ all have color $\neq \alpha$.

Now, on the vertical line $x = 1$, the 6 points $(1, y), \ldots, (1, y+5)$ are colored with 2 colors (not $\alpha$). By pigeonhole, one of these 2 colors appears $\geq 3$ times among the 6 points. Say color $\beta$ appears at positions $y + b_1, y + b_2, y + b_3$.

Now, consider the condition from $L_0 = \langle (3, 0), (0, 2) \rangle$: no mono $(x, y), (x+3, y), (x, y+2)$. If $c(1, y+b_i) = c(4, y+b_i) = \beta$ and $c(1, y+b_i+2) = \beta$, then we have a mono triangle. So either $c(4, y+b_i) \neq \beta$ or $c(1, y+b_i+2) \neq \beta$.

This is getting very complicated. Let me try a different approach.

**Actually, let me try to use the result from the literature.**

I believe this problem is related to the following result:

**Theorem:** For any $r$-coloring of $\mathbb{Z}^2$ and any positive integer $n$, there exists a monochromatic triangle of area $n/2$ if and only if $n$ is not avoidable.

The key result I need is:

**Theorem (possibly from the polymath project):** For 3-colorings of $\mathbb{Z}^2$, the minimum forced area is $S = 3$.

Let me try to prove this using the approach I've been developing.

**Proof sketch:**

1. **Lower bound ($S \geq 3$):** For each $s \in \{1/2, 1, 3/2, 2, 5/2\}$, exhibit a 3-coloring avoiding area $s$.
   - $s = 1/2, 1, 2, 5/2$: avoided by $c(x,y) = x \mod 3$.
   - $s = 3/2$: avoided by $c(x,y) = (x \bmod 2) + (y \bmod 2)$.

2. **Upper bound ($S \leq 3$):** Show that every 3-coloring of $\mathbb{Z}^2$ contains a monochromatic triangle of area 3.

For the upper bound, I'll use the following approach:

Consider any 3-coloring of $\mathbb{Z}^2$. Consider the 12 sublattices of index 6. For each sublattice $L_k = \langle u_k, v_k \rangle$, the condition for avoiding area 3 is that no coset of $L_k$ contains a monochromatic triangle $(P, P+u_k, P+v_k)$.

**Key claim:** No 3-coloring can simultaneously avoid monochromatic triangles for all 12 sublattices.

To prove this, I'll show that the conditions from different sublattices are contradictory.

**Proof of key claim:**

Consider the 6 sublattices $L_b = \langle (1, b), (0, 6) \rangle$ for $b = 0, 1, \ldots, 5$.

For each $b$, the condition is: for all $(x, y)$, the three points $(x, y), (x+1, y+b), (x, y+6)$ are not monochromatic.

Now, fix $x$ and consider the vertical line at $x$. The coloring on this line is a 3-coloring of $\mathbb{Z}$. By van der Waerden's theorem, there exist arbitrarily long monochromatic arithmetic progressions. In particular, there exist $y_0$ and $d$ such that $(x, y_0), (x, y_0+d), (x, y_0+2d)$ are all the same color, say color $\alpha$.

Hmm, but I need to relate this to the sublattice conditions. Let me think more.

Actually, let me try a more direct approach.

**Direct proof using a finite configuration:**

I need to find a finite set $F$ such that any 3-coloring of $F$ contains a mono triangle of area 3.

From my earlier analysis, the 7-point set $\{(0,0), (3,0), (6,0), (0,2), (3,2), (6,2), (3,1)\}$ doesn't work (I found a valid coloring). Let me try adding more points.

Let me add the points $(0,1)$ and $(6,1)$ to get a 9-point set:
$F = \{(0,0), (3,0), (6,0), (0,1), (3,1), (6,1), (0,2), (3,2), (6,2)\}$

This is a $3 \times 3$ grid with spacing $(3, 1)$: points at $(3i, j)$ for $i \in \{0,1,2\}, j \in \{0,1,2\}$.

The area-3 triangles in this set: $D = 3i_1(j_2 - j_3) + 3i_2(j_3 - j_1) + 3i_3(j_1 - j_2) = 3[i_1(j_2 - j_3) + i_2(j_3 - j_1) + i_3(j_1 - j_2)]$. So $D = 3D'$ where $D' = i_1(j_2 - j_3) + i_2(j_3 - j_1) + i_3(j_1 - j_2)$. For $|D| = 6$: $|D'| = 2$.

The possible values of $D'$ for $i_k \in \{0,1,2\}, j_k \in \{0,1,2\}$: $D'$ ranges from $-4$ to $4$ (max when $i = (0, 2, 0), j = (0, 0, 2)$: $D' = 0 + 2 \cdot 2 + 0 = 4$). So $|D'| = 2$ is achievable.

The triangles with $|D'| = 2$ (area 3 in original coordinates):
- $i = (0, 1, 0), j = (0, 0, 2)$: $D' = 0 + 1 \cdot 2 + 0 = 2$. Points: $(0,0), (3,0), (0,2)$. ✓
- $i = (0, 1, 0), j = (0, 2, 0)$: $D' = 0 + 1 \cdot (-2) + 0 = -2$. Points: $(0,0), (3,2), (0,0)$... wait, $(0,0)$ appears twice. Let me be more careful.

$D' = i_1(j_2 - j_3) + i_2(j_3 - j_1) + i_3(j_1 - j_2)$.

For $i = (0, 1, 2), j = (0, 0, 1)$: $D' = 0(0-1) + 1(1-0) + 2(0-0) = 1$. $|D'| = 1$. Area = 3/2.
For $i = (0, 1, 2), j = (0, 1, 0)$: $D' = 0(1-0) + 1(0-0) + 2(0-1) = -2$. $|D'| = 2$. Area = 3. ✓ Points: $(0,0), (3,1), (6,0)$.
For $i = (0, 1, 2), j = (0, 0, 2)$: $D' = 0(0-2) + 1(2-0) + 2(0-0) = 2$. Area = 3. ✓ Points: $(0,0), (3,0), (6,2)$... wait, $(0,0), (3,0), (6,2)$: $D = 0(0-2) + 3(2-0) + 6(0-0) = 6$. Area = 3. ✓
For $i = (0, 2, 1), j = (0, 0, 2)$: $D' = 0 + 2(2-0) + 1(0-0) = 4$. Area = 6.
For $i = (0, 2, 0), j = (0, 1, 0)$: $D' = 0(1-0) + 2(0-0) + 0(0-1) = 0$. Degenerate.
For $i = (1, 0, 2), j = (0, 1, 2)$: $D' = 1(1-2) + 0(2-0) + 2(0-1) = -1 - 2 = -3$. Area = 9/2.
For $i = (0, 1, 0), j = (0, 2, 1)$: $D' = 0(2-1) + 1(1-0) + 0(0-2) = 1$. Area = 3/2.
For $i = (0, 2, 1), j = (0, 1, 0)$: $D' = 0(1-0) + 2(0-0) + 1(0-1) = -1$. Area = 3/
